Predicting Spin-Dependent Coulomb Interaction Based on the Yang-Mills Equations
Jiang-Lin Zhou, Choo Hiap Oh, Jing-Ling Chen

Abstract
The standard Coulomb interaction is one of four fundamental interactions in Nature. It is interesting to know how will the standard Coulomb interaction be modified when it meets spin. Since the standard Coulomb potential is a simple but fundamental solution of Maxwell’s equations, hence Maxwell’s equations can predict the existence of the standard Coulomb potential. The Yang-Mills equations are the natural generalizations of Maxwell’s equations from the Abelian potentials to the non-Abelian ones, thus based on the Yang-Mills equations, one can predict the reasonable form of spin-dependent Coulomb potential, which naturally reduces the standard Coulomb potential if without considering the spin. Our work sheds a new light to how to couple spin with fundamental interactions.



I
Introduction

It is well-known that there are four fundamental interactions in our Nature. They are electromagnetic interaction, weak interaction, strong interaction, and gravitational interaction, respectively. Maxwell’s theory well describes the electromagnetic interaction
1999Jackson
. As a natural generalization of Maxwell’s theory, the Yang-Mills theory has successfully unified the first three fundamental interactions, thus becoming one of cornerstones in modern physics
yang_conservation_1954
.

Given two particles, particle 1 has electric charge
Q_{1}
and particle has electric charge
Q_{2}
, then the usual Coulomb force between them is given by

\displaystyle\vec{\mathbb{F}}_{\rm cl}=\dfrac{Q_{1}Q_{2}}{r^{2}},

(1)

where
r
is the distance between two particles. Similar to the gravitational force, the usual Coulomb force belongs to the long-range forces. It is well-known that particles can possess internal degrees of freedom, such as
spin
and
isospin
. It is interesting to ask a question: What will happen if these fundamental interactions meet
spin
? Actually, scientists have started to investigate some spin-dependent forces
peres1978
;
Wilczek1984
, such as spin-dependent macroscopic forces from new particle exchange
Dobrescu2006
;
Fadeev2019
;
Du2023
and spin-dependent gravitational interaction
Tarallo2014
;
Duan2016
;
Zhang2023
.

In classical physics, the Coulomb interaction is represented by Eq. (
1
) from the viewpoint of
force
. However, in quantum mechanics, the concept of
force
becomes ambiguous, thus instead the Coulomb interaction is represented from the viewpoint of
potential
. The Coulomb potential is given by

\displaystyle\varphi_{\rm cl}=\dfrac{\kappa}{r},

(2)

with
\kappa
being a constant. One may observe that Eq. (
1
) and Eq. (
2
) are spin-independent. This gives rise to a natural question: How will the usual Coulomb interaction be modified when it meets spin? The purpose of this work is to predict or derive the reasonable spin-dependent Coulomb interaction (SDCI) based on the Yang-Mills equations. The logic of the prediction is illustrated in Fig.
1
.

Fig. 1:
Illustration of Predicting Spin-Dependent Coulomb Interaction Based on the Yang-Mills Equations
. The standard Coulomb potential is a simple but fundamental solution of Maxwell’s equations, hence Maxwell’s equations can predict the existence of the standard Coulomb potential. The Yang-Mills equations are the natural generalizations of Maxwell’s equations from the Abelian potentials to the non-Abelian ones, and the non-Abelian operators can be realized by spin operators, then one can expect that there will be a counterpart solution of potential that depends on spin. Thus based on the Yang-Mills equations, one can predict the reasonable form of spin-dependent Coulomb potential, which naturally reduces the standard Coulomb potential if without considering the spin.

The work is organized as follows. In Sec. II, we make a brief review of the Yang-Mills equations. In Sec. III, we demonstrate that the Coulomb-type potential is a simple but fundamental solution of Maxwell’s equations, and the Coulomb potential can be used to bind two particles. In Sec. IV, we derive the spin-dependent Coulomb potential, which is an exact solution of the Yang-Mills equations. In Sec. V, we solve Schrö equation with the
spin-dependent Coulomb interaction. In Sec. VI, we Solve Dirac’s equation with the spin-dependent Coulomb interaction. Some discussions related to spin forces are made in Sec. VII.

II
The Yang-Mills Equations

The Yang-Mills theory is one of cornerstones in modern physics. The Yang-Mills equations in vacuum without sources (i.e., the 4-vector current
{J^{\nu}}=0
) are given by

\displaystyle D_{\mu}{F}^{\mu\nu}\equiv\partial_{\mu}{F}^{\mu\nu}-{\rm i}\,g\Bigl[\mathbb{A}_{\mu},\ {F}^{\mu\nu}\Bigr]=0,

(3a)

\displaystyle D_{\mu}{F}_{\nu\gamma}+D_{\nu}{F}_{\gamma\mu}+D_{\gamma}{F}_{\mu\nu}=0,

(3b)

where the field-strength tensor is defined by

{F}_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}\mathbb{A}_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}\mathbb{A}_{\mu}+\mathrm{i}\,g[\mathbb{A}_{\mu},\ \mathbb{A}_{\nu}],\;\;\;\;\;(\mu,\nu=0,1,2,3),

(4)

g
is the coupling parameter, and
\mathbb{A}
’s are the potentials. Note that we have used the four-vectorial notations
x=(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
, with
x_{1}\equiv x
,
x_{2}\equiv y
,
x_{3}\equiv z
,
x_{0}\equiv c\,t
, and

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{\mu}=(\varphi,-\vec{\mathcal{A}})=(\varphi,-\mathcal{A}_{x},-\mathcal{A}_{y},-\mathcal{A}_{z}),\;\;\;\;\;{\partial}_{\mu}=\left(\frac{1}{c}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial t},\vec{\nabla}\right),\;\;\;\;\;\vec{\nabla}=(\dfrac{\partial}{\partial x},\dfrac{\partial}{\partial y},\dfrac{\partial}{\partial z}).

(5)

Here
\varphi
is called the
scalar potential
, and
\vec{\mathcal{A}}
is called the
vector potential
.
The Yang-Mills equations are the natural generalization of Maxwell’s equations from the Abelian potentials to the non-Abelian ones. When the commutator
[\mathbb{A}_{\mu},\mathbb{A}_{\nu}]=0
, the definition of
{F}_{\mu\nu}
reduces to the Abelian case (i.e.,
F_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}\mathbb{A}_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}\mathbb{A}_{\mu}
for the situation of Maxwell’s equations). When some of the commutators
[\mathbb{A}_{\mu},\mathbb{A}_{\nu}]
are not zero, then one says that the potentials are non-Abelian.

The matrix form of field tensor
F_{\mu\nu}
is given by
1999Jackson

F_{\mu\nu}=\begin{bmatrix}0&\mathcal{E}_{x}&\mathcal{E}_{y}&\mathcal{E}_{z}\\
-\mathcal{E}_{x}&0&-\mathcal{B}_{z}&\mathcal{B}_{y}\\
-\mathcal{E}_{y}&\mathcal{B}_{z}&0&-\mathcal{B}_{x}\\
-\mathcal{E}_{z}&-\mathcal{B}_{y}&\mathcal{B}_{x}&0\end{bmatrix},

(6)

or

{F}^{\mu\nu}=\begin{bmatrix}0&-\mathcal{E}_{x}&-\mathcal{E}_{y}&-\mathcal{E}_{z}\\
\mathcal{E}_{x}&0&-\mathcal{B}_{z}&\mathcal{B}_{y}\\
\mathcal{E}_{y}&\mathcal{B}_{z}&0&-\mathcal{B}_{x}\\
\mathcal{E}_{z}&-\mathcal{B}_{y}&\mathcal{B}_{x}&0\end{bmatrix},

(7)

Based on which, we have the three components of “magnetic” field
\vec{\mathcal{B}}=\left(\mathcal{B}_{x},\mathcal{B}_{y},\mathcal{B}_{z}\right)
as

\begin{split}\mathcal{B}_{x}=&\ F_{32}=\dfrac{\partial\,\mathbb{A}_{2}}{\partial\,x_{3}}-\dfrac{\partial\,\mathbb{A}_{3}}{\partial\,x_{2}}+\mathrm{i}\,g[\mathbb{A}_{3},\mathbb{A}_{2}]=-\dfrac{\partial\,\mathcal{A}_{y}}{\partial\,z}+\dfrac{\partial\,\mathcal{A}_{z}}{\partial\,y}+\mathrm{i}\,g[\mathcal{A}_{z},\mathcal{A}_{y}]=\left[\dfrac{\partial\mathcal{A}_{z}}{\partial y}-\dfrac{\partial\mathcal{A}_{y}}{\partial z}\right]-\mathrm{i}\,g\left[\mathcal{A}_{y},\mathcal{A}_{z}\right],\\
\mathcal{B}_{y}=&\ F_{13}=\dfrac{\partial\,\mathbb{A}_{3}}{\partial\,x_{1}}-\dfrac{\partial\,\mathbb{A}_{1}}{\partial\,x_{3}}+\mathrm{i}\,g[\mathbb{A}_{1},\mathbb{A}_{3}]=-\dfrac{\partial\,\mathcal{A}_{z}}{\partial\,x}+\dfrac{\partial\,\mathcal{A}_{x}}{\partial\,z}+\mathrm{i}\,g[\mathcal{A}_{x},\mathcal{A}_{z}]=\left[\dfrac{\partial\mathcal{A}_{x}}{\partial z}-\dfrac{\partial\mathcal{A}_{z}}{\partial x}\right]-\mathrm{i}\,g\left[\mathcal{A}_{z},\mathcal{A}_{x}\right],\\
\mathcal{B}_{z}=&\ F_{21}=\dfrac{\partial\,\mathbb{A}_{1}}{\partial\,x_{2}}-\dfrac{\partial\,\mathbb{A}_{2}}{\partial\,x_{1}}+\mathrm{i}\,g[\mathbb{A}_{2},\mathbb{A}_{1}]=-\dfrac{\partial\,\mathcal{A}_{x}}{\partial\,y}+\dfrac{\partial\,\mathcal{A}_{y}}{\partial\,x}+\mathrm{i}\,g[\mathcal{A}_{y},\mathcal{A}_{x}]=\left[\dfrac{\partial\mathcal{A}_{y}}{\partial x}-\dfrac{\partial\mathcal{A}_{x}}{\partial y}\right]-\mathrm{i}\,g\left[\mathcal{A}_{x},\mathcal{A}_{y}\right],\end{split}

(8)

and the three components of “electric” field
\vec{\mathcal{E}}=\left(\mathcal{E}_{x},\mathcal{E}_{y},\mathcal{E}_{z}\right)
as

\begin{split}\mathcal{E}_{x}=&\ F_{01}=\dfrac{\partial\,\mathbb{A}_{1}}{\partial\,x_{0}}-\dfrac{\partial\,\mathbb{A}_{0}}{\partial\,x_{1}}+\mathrm{i}\,g[\mathbb{A}_{0},\mathbb{A}_{1}]=-\frac{1}{c}\dfrac{\partial\,\mathcal{A}_{x}}{\partial\,t}-\dfrac{\partial\,\varphi}{\partial\,x}-\mathrm{i}\,g[\varphi,\ \mathcal{A}_{x}],\\
\mathcal{E}_{y}=&\ F_{02}=\dfrac{\partial\,\mathbb{A}_{2}}{\partial\,x_{0}}-\dfrac{\partial\,\mathbb{A}_{0}}{\partial\,x_{2}}+\mathrm{i}\,g[\mathbb{A}_{0},\mathbb{A}_{2}]=-\frac{1}{c}\dfrac{\partial\,\mathcal{A}_{y}}{\partial\,t}-\dfrac{\partial\,\varphi}{\partial\,y}-\mathrm{i}\,g[\varphi,\ \mathcal{A}_{y}],\\
\mathcal{E}_{z}=&\ F_{03}=\dfrac{\partial\,\mathbb{A}_{3}}{\partial\,x_{0}}-\dfrac{\partial\,\mathbb{A}_{0}}{\partial\,x_{3}}+\mathrm{i}\,g[\mathbb{A}_{0},\mathbb{A}_{3}]=-\frac{1}{c}\dfrac{\partial\,\mathcal{A}_{z}}{\partial\,t}-\dfrac{\partial\,\varphi}{\partial\,z}-\mathrm{i}\,g[\varphi,\ \mathcal{A}_{z}].\end{split}

(9)

According to Eq. (
8
) and Eq. (
9
), one may write down the vector forms of “magnetic” field and “electric” field as

\displaystyle\vec{\mathcal{B}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{\mathcal{A}}-{\rm i}\,g\left(\vec{\mathcal{A}}\times\vec{\mathcal{A}}\right),

(10a)

\displaystyle\vec{\mathcal{E}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\dfrac{1}{c}\dfrac{\partial\,\vec{\mathcal{A}}}{\partial\,t}-\vec{\nabla}\varphi-{\rm i}\,g\left[\varphi,\ \vec{\mathcal{A}}\right].

(10b)

Then in terms of
\big\{\vec{\mathcal{E}},\vec{\mathcal{B}},\vec{\mathcal{A}},\varphi\big\}
, the Yang-Mills equations can be recast to the following form

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{\mathcal{E}}\ +\boxed{{\rm i}\,g\Bigl(\vec{\mathcal{A}}\cdot\vec{\mathcal{E}}-\vec{\mathcal{E}}\cdot\vec{\mathcal{A}}\Bigr)}=0,

(11a)

\displaystyle-\dfrac{1}{c}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\,t}\vec{\mathcal{B}}-\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{\mathcal{E}}+\boxed{{\rm i}\,g\biggl(\Bigl[\varphi,\ \vec{\mathcal{B}}\Bigr]-\vec{\mathcal{A}}\times\vec{\mathcal{E}}-\vec{\mathcal{E}}\times\vec{\mathcal{A}}\biggr)}=0,

(11b)

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{\mathcal{B}}\ +\boxed{{\rm i}\,g\Bigl(\vec{\mathcal{A}}\cdot\vec{\mathcal{B}}-\vec{\mathcal{B}}\cdot\vec{\mathcal{A}}\Bigr)}=0,

(11c)

\displaystyle-\dfrac{1}{c}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\,t}\vec{\mathcal{E}}+\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{\mathcal{B}}+\boxed{{\rm i}\,g\biggl(\Bigl[\varphi,\ \vec{\mathcal{E}}\Bigr]+\vec{\mathcal{A}}\times\vec{\mathcal{B}}+\vec{\mathcal{B}}\times\vec{\mathcal{A}}\biggr)}=0,

(11d)

where the “boxed” expressions imply the self-interaction terms regarding to potentials
\big\{\vec{\mathcal{A}},\varphi\big\}
and fields
\big\{\vec{\mathcal{E}},\vec{\mathcal{B}}\big\}
in the Yang-Mills equations. The “boxed” terms come from the nonlinear terms in Eqs. (
3a
) and (
3b
), such as
{\rm i}\,g[\mathbb{A}_{\mu},\ {F}^{\mu\nu}]
. Note that Yang-Mills equations are both Lorentz covariant and local gauge covariant. Generally, due to the self-interaction terms, it is hard to obtain the exact solutions for the Yang-Mills equations.

If one neglects the “boxed” terms (or let
g=0
), then the above expressions are just the usual forms of Maxwell’s equations (in vacuum without sources). Namely,

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{\mathcal{E}}=0,

(12a)

\displaystyle-\dfrac{1}{c}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\,t}\vec{\mathcal{B}}-\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{\mathcal{E}}=0,

(12b)

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{\mathcal{B}}=0,

(12c)

\displaystyle-\dfrac{1}{c}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\,t}\vec{\mathcal{E}}+\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{\mathcal{B}}=0.

(12d)

III
Coulomb-Type Potential: A Simple but Fundamental Solution of Maxwell’s equations

The Coulomb-type potential is given by

\displaystyle\varphi_{\rm cl}=\dfrac{\kappa}{r},

(13)

where
\kappa
is a constant. A significant feature of Coulomb-type potential is that
\varphi_{\rm cl}(r)
is proportional to
\frac{1}{r}
. Consider two charged particles, particle 1 has a charge
q
and particle 2 has a charge
Q
. When
\kappa
is taken as

\displaystyle\kappa=Q,

(14)

then

\displaystyle V(r)=q\varphi_{\rm cl}=\dfrac{qQ}{r}

(15)

is just the standard Coulomb potential in electromagnetics, and
V(r)
represents the standard Coulomb interaction between two charged particles. Surprisingly, in our nature, both the standard Coulomb potential in electromagnetics and the gravitational potential in Newtonian gravity theory share the same form of Coulomb-type potential shown in Eq. (
13
).

It gives rise to a natural question: Can one derive the Coulomb-type potential based on some certain fundamental theories? The answer is positive.
Actually the Coulomb-type potential is a simple but fundamental solution of Maxwell’s equations. The result can be expressed in the following theorem.

Theorem 1
.

Let the vector potential and the scalar potential be

\displaystyle\vec{\mathcal{A}}=0,\;\;\;\;\;\varphi=\dfrac{\kappa}{r},

(16)

then the set of potentials
\{\vec{\mathcal{A}},\varphi\}
is a solution of Maxwell’s equations.

Proof.

By substituting Eq. (
16
) into Eq. (
10a
) and Eq. (
10b
), we have the magnetic filed and the electric field as

\displaystyle\vec{\mathcal{B}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{\mathcal{A}}-{\rm i}\,g\bigl(\vec{\mathcal{A}}\times\vec{\mathcal{A}}\bigr)=0,

(17a)

\displaystyle\vec{\mathcal{E}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\dfrac{1}{c}\dfrac{\partial\,\vec{\mathcal{A}}}{\partial\,t}-\vec{\nabla}\varphi-{\rm i}\,g\left[\varphi,\ \vec{\mathcal{A}}\right]=-\vec{\nabla}\varphi

(17b)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\dfrac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(\dfrac{\kappa}{r}\right)\hat{e}_{x}-\dfrac{\partial}{\partial y}\left(\dfrac{\kappa}{r}\right)\hat{e}_{y}-\dfrac{\partial}{\partial z}\left(\dfrac{\kappa}{r}\right)\hat{e}_{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\dfrac{\partial}{\partial r}\left(\dfrac{\kappa}{r}\right)\dfrac{x}{r}\hat{e}_{x}-\dfrac{\partial}{\partial r}\left(\dfrac{\kappa}{r}\right)\dfrac{y}{r}\hat{e}_{y}-\dfrac{\partial}{\partial r}\left(\dfrac{\kappa}{r}\right)\dfrac{z}{r}\hat{e}_{z}=\dfrac{\kappa}{r^{2}}\hat{e}_{r},

with

\displaystyle\hat{e}_{r}=\dfrac{\vec{r}}{r}.

(18)

Because
\{\vec{\mathcal{A}},\varphi\}
is time-independent, thus
\vec{\mathcal{B}}
and
\vec{\mathcal{E}}
are static fields. Let us come to check Maxwell’s equations as shown in Eq. (
12a
)-Eq. (
12d
). Because
\vec{\mathcal{B}}=0
and
\vec{\mathcal{E}}
is time-independent, then Eq. (
12c
) and Eq. (
12d
) are automatically satisfied. Accordingly, Eq. (
12a
) and Eq. (
12b
) become

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{\mathcal{E}}=0,

(19a)

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{\mathcal{E}}=0.

(19b)

We can have

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{\mathcal{E}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\cdot\left(\dfrac{\kappa}{r^{2}}\hat{e}_{r}\right)=\kappa\,\vec{\nabla}\cdot\left(\dfrac{x}{r^{3}}\hat{e}_{x}+\dfrac{y}{r^{3}}\hat{e}_{y}+\dfrac{z}{r^{3}}\hat{e}_{z}\right)=\kappa\,\left[\dfrac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(\dfrac{x}{r^{3}}\right)+\dfrac{\partial}{\partial y}\left(\dfrac{y}{r^{3}}\right)+\dfrac{\partial}{\partial z}\left(\dfrac{z}{r^{3}}\right)\right]

(20)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\kappa\left[\left(\dfrac{1}{r^{3}}-3\dfrac{x^{2}}{r^{5}}\right)+\left(\dfrac{1}{r^{3}}-3\dfrac{y^{2}}{r^{5}}\right)+\left(\dfrac{1}{r^{3}}-3\dfrac{z^{2}}{r^{5}}\right)\right]=\kappa\left[\dfrac{3}{r^{3}}-3\dfrac{x^{2}+y^{2}+z^{2}}{r^{5}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\kappa\left[\dfrac{3}{r^{3}}-3\dfrac{1}{r^{3}}\right]=0,

thus Eq. (
19a
) is satisfied. Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{\mathcal{E}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\times\left(\dfrac{\kappa}{r^{2}}\hat{e}_{r}\right)=\kappa\,\vec{\nabla}\times\left(\dfrac{x}{r^{3}}\hat{e}_{x}+\dfrac{y}{r^{3}}\hat{e}_{y}+\dfrac{z}{r^{3}}\hat{e}_{z}\right)

(21)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\kappa\,\left[\left(\dfrac{\partial\left(\dfrac{z}{r^{3}}\right)}{\partial y}-\dfrac{\partial\left(\dfrac{y}{r^{3}}\right)}{\partial z}\right)\hat{e}_{x}+\left(\dfrac{\partial\left(\dfrac{x}{r^{3}}\right)}{\partial z}-\dfrac{\partial\left(\dfrac{z}{r^{3}}\right)}{\partial x}\right)\hat{e}_{y}+\left(\dfrac{\partial\left(\dfrac{y}{r^{3}}\right)}{\partial x}-\dfrac{\partial\left(\dfrac{x}{r^{3}}\right)}{\partial y}\right)\hat{e}_{z}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\kappa\,\left[\left(-3\dfrac{yz}{r^{5}}+3\dfrac{zy}{r^{5}}\right)\hat{e}_{x}+\left(-3\dfrac{zx}{r^{5}}+3\dfrac{xz}{r^{5}}\right)\hat{e}_{y}+\left(-3\dfrac{xy}{r^{5}}+3\dfrac{yx}{r^{5}}\right)\hat{e}_{z}\right]=0,

thus Eq. (
19b
) is satisfied. This ends the proof.
∎

Undoubtly, the Coulomb-type potential represents a kind of fundamental interactions in nature. In the following, let us study this fundamental interaction from the angle of view of classical picture as well as quantum picture.

III.1
Classical Picture: Binding Two Particles with Coulomb Potential

Suppose there are two particles, i.e., particle 1 and particle 2. The first particle (e.g, an electron) has a charge
q
(e.g.,
q=-e=-|e|
, and
e=|e|
is the unit of electric charge) and a mass
M
, and the second particle (e.g., atomic nucleus) has a charge
Q
(e.g.,
Q=Ze
,
Z
is the number of protons, and
Z=1
for a single proton). The particle 2 induces a scalar potential
\varphi=\dfrac{\kappa}{r}=\dfrac{Q}{r}
and hence a static electric field
\vec{\mathcal{E}}=\dfrac{\kappa}{r^{2}}\hat{e}_{r}
. Due to the electric filed, particle 2 exerts a force on particle 1, and the expression of the force is given by

\displaystyle\vec{\mathbb{F}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q\vec{\mathcal{E}}=\dfrac{q\kappa}{r^{2}}\hat{e}_{r}=\dfrac{qQ}{r^{2}}\hat{e}_{r}.

(22)

Here
\vec{\mathbb{F}}
is the standard Coulomb force in electromagnetics. It is a kind of central forces. If
q\kappa<0
, then
\vec{\mathbb{F}}
is an attractive force (or the centripetal force); and if
q\kappa>0
, then
\vec{\mathbb{F}}
is a repulsive force.

Now, under the action of Coulomb force
\vec{\mathbb{F}}
, we come to determine the equation of motion of
\vec{r}
for particle 1. From Newton’s second law, we have

\displaystyle\vec{\mathbb{F}}=M\vec{a}=M\dfrac{{\rm d}^{2}\vec{r}}{{\rm d}t^{2}}.

(23)

For simplicity, we consider the polar coordinate system (i.e., a two-dimensional plan). The particle 2 is located on the origin of the polar coordinate system, and the position of particle 1 is denoted by
\vec{r}=(r\cos\theta,r\sin\theta)
.
With the help of

\displaystyle{\rm d}\hat{e}_{r}=\theta\,\hat{e}_{\theta},\;\;\;\;\;{\rm d}\hat{e}_{\theta}=-\theta\,\hat{e}_{r},

\displaystyle\dfrac{{\rm d}\hat{e}_{r}}{{\rm d}t}=\dfrac{{\rm d}\left(\theta\,\hat{e}_{\theta}\right)}{{\rm d}t}=\dfrac{{\rm d}\theta}{{\rm d}t}\,\hat{e}_{\theta}=\dot{\theta}\,\hat{e}_{\theta},\;\;\;\;\;\dfrac{{\rm d}\hat{e}_{\theta}}{{\rm d}t}=\dfrac{{\rm d}\left(-\theta\,\hat{e}_{r}\right)}{{\rm d}t}=-\dfrac{{\rm d}\theta}{{\rm d}t}\,\hat{e}_{r}=-\dot{\theta}\,\hat{e}_{r},

(24)

we have

\displaystyle\dfrac{{\rm d}\vec{r}}{{\rm d}t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{{\rm d}\left(r\hat{e}_{r}\right)}{{\rm d}t}=\dfrac{{\rm d}r}{{\rm d}t}\hat{e}_{r}+r\dfrac{{\rm d}\hat{e}_{r}}{{\rm d}t}=\dot{r}\hat{e}_{r}+r\dot{\theta}\,\hat{e}_{\theta},

(25)

\displaystyle\dfrac{{\rm d}^{2}\vec{r}}{{\rm d}t^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{{\rm d}\left(\dot{r}\hat{e}_{r}+r\dot{\theta}\,\hat{e}_{\theta}\right)}{{\rm d}t}=\ddot{r}\hat{e}_{r}+\dot{r}\dfrac{{\rm d}\hat{e}_{r}}{{\rm d}t}+\dot{r}\dot{\theta}\hat{e}_{\theta}+r\ddot{\theta}\hat{e}_{\theta}+r\dot{\theta}\dfrac{{\rm d}\hat{e}_{\theta}}{{\rm d}t}

(26)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ddot{r}\hat{e}_{r}+\dot{r}\dot{\theta}\,\hat{e}_{\theta}+\dot{r}\dot{\theta}\hat{e}_{\theta}+r\ddot{\theta}\hat{e}_{\theta}+r\dot{\theta}\left(-\dot{\theta}\,\hat{e}_{r}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\ddot{r}-r\dot{\theta}^{2}\right)\hat{e}_{r}+\left(r\ddot{\theta}+2\dot{r}\dot{\theta}\right)\hat{e}_{\theta}.

From

\displaystyle\vec{\mathbb{F}}=M\dfrac{{\rm d}^{2}\vec{r}}{{\rm d}t^{2}}=\dfrac{qQ}{r^{2}}\,\hat{e}_{r},

(27)

we have

\displaystyle M\ddot{r}-Mr\dot{\theta}^{2}=|\vec{\mathbb{F}}|=\dfrac{qQ}{r^{2}},

(28)

and

\displaystyle M\left(r\ddot{\theta}+2\dot{r}\dot{\theta}\right)=0.

(29)

From Eq. (
29
), one has

\displaystyle r\,M\left(r\ddot{\theta}+2\dot{r}\dot{\theta}\right)=M\left(r^{2}\ddot{\theta}+2r\,\dot{r}\dot{\theta}\right)=\dfrac{{\rm d}\left(mr^{2}\dot{\theta}\right)}{{\rm d}t}=0,

(30)

which means the angular momentum is a conserved quantity, i.e.,

\displaystyle\ell=Mr^{2}\dot{\theta}={\rm constant}.

(31)

Based on which, we have

\displaystyle\dot{\theta}=\dfrac{{\rm d}\theta}{{\rm d}t}=\dfrac{\ell}{Mr^{2}},

(32)

thus

\displaystyle\dfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}=\dfrac{{\rm d}\theta}{{\rm d}t}\dfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\theta}=\dfrac{\ell}{Mr^{2}}\dfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\theta}.

(33)

Then Eq. (
28
) becomes

\displaystyle M\dfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\left[\dfrac{{\rm d}r}{{\rm d}t}\right]-Mr\dot{\theta}^{2}=\dfrac{qQ}{r^{2}},

(34)

i.e.,

\displaystyle M\dfrac{\ell}{mr^{2}}\dfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\theta}\left[\dfrac{\ell}{Mr^{2}}\dfrac{{\rm d}r}{{\rm d}\theta}\right]-Mr\dot{\theta}^{2}=\dfrac{qQ}{r^{2}},

(35)

i.e.,

\displaystyle\dfrac{\ell^{2}}{Mr^{2}}\dfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\theta}\left[\dfrac{1}{r^{2}}\dfrac{{\rm d}r}{{\rm d}\theta}\right]-Mr\dot{\theta}^{2}=\dfrac{qQ}{r^{2}}.

(36)

We now let

\displaystyle u=\dfrac{1}{r},

(37)

then we have

\displaystyle\dfrac{{\rm d}r}{{\rm d}\theta}=\dfrac{{\rm d}r}{{\rm d}u}\dfrac{{\rm d}u}{{\rm d}\theta}=-\dfrac{1}{u^{2}}\dfrac{{\rm d}u}{{\rm d}\theta}.

(38)

Then Eq. (
36
) becomes

\displaystyle\dfrac{\ell^{2}}{Mr^{2}}\dfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\theta}\left[\dfrac{1}{r^{2}}\left(-\dfrac{1}{u^{2}}\dfrac{{\rm d}u}{{\rm d}\theta}\right)\right]-Mr\dot{\theta}^{2}=\dfrac{qQ}{r^{2}},

(39)

i.e.,

\displaystyle-\dfrac{\ell^{2}}{Mr^{2}}\dfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\theta}\left[\dfrac{1}{r^{2}}\left(\dfrac{1}{u^{2}}\dfrac{{\rm d}u}{{\rm d}\theta}\right)\right]-\dfrac{1}{Mr^{3}}\left(Mr^{2}\dot{\theta}\right)^{2}=\dfrac{qQ}{r^{2}},

(40)

i.e.,

\displaystyle-\dfrac{\ell^{2}u^{2}}{M}\dfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\theta}\left[u^{2}\left(\dfrac{1}{u^{2}}\dfrac{{\rm d}u}{{\rm d}\theta}\right)\right]-\dfrac{\ell^{2}u^{3}}{M}=qQu^{2},

(41)

i.e.,

\displaystyle-\dfrac{\ell^{2}u^{2}}{M}\dfrac{{\rm d}^{2}u}{{\rm d}\theta^{2}}-\dfrac{\ell^{2}u^{3}}{M}=qQu^{2}=q\kappa u^{2},

(42)

i.e.,

\displaystyle-\dfrac{\ell^{2}}{M}\dfrac{{\rm d}^{2}u}{{\rm d}\theta^{2}}-\dfrac{\ell^{2}}{M}u=q\kappa,

(43)

i.e.,

\displaystyle\dfrac{{\rm d}^{2}u}{{\rm d}\theta^{2}}+u=-\dfrac{Mq\kappa}{\ell^{2}}.

(44)

The solution of Eq. (
44
) is

\displaystyle u=-\dfrac{Mq\kappa}{\ell^{2}}+C_{1}\cos(\theta-\theta_{0})=\dfrac{M{|q\kappa|}}{\ell^{2}}\left[1+{\rm e}\cos(\theta-\theta_{0})\right],

(45)

here we have considered
\vec{\mathbb{F}}
as the attractive force, thus
q\kappa=-|q\kappa|<0
. Then the equation of motion of
\vec{r}
is given by

\displaystyle r=\dfrac{\ell^{2}}{M{|q\kappa|}}\dfrac{1}{\left[1+{\rm e}\cos(\theta-\theta_{0})\right]},

(46)

which is an equation of the conic section, with
{\rm e}
being the eccentricity ratio. When
{\rm e}>1
, the trajectory of particle 1 is a hyperbola; when
{\rm e}=1
, the trajectory of particle 1 is a parabola; when
0\leq{\rm e}<1
, the trajectory of particle 1 is an ellipse (specially
{\rm e}=0
corresponds to a circle).

Remark 1
.

The Relation between Eccentricity Ratio
{\rm e}
and Energy
. Classically, the energy of the system can be written as

\displaystyle E=\frac{p^{2}}{2M}+q\varphi=\frac{1}{2}Mv^{2}+\dfrac{q\kappa}{r}.

(47)

Because the square of velocity is

v^{2}=\dot{r}^{2}+r^{2}\dot{\theta}^{2},

(48)

and the angular momentum is

\ell=Mr^{2}\dot{\theta},

(49)

then the energy becomes

\displaystyle E=\frac{1}{2}Mv^{2}+\dfrac{q\kappa}{r}=\frac{1}{2}M\left(\dot{r}^{2}+\frac{\ell^{2}}{M^{2}r^{2}}\right)+\dfrac{q\kappa}{r},

(50)

i.e.,

\displaystyle\dot{r}^{2}=\dfrac{2}{M}\left(E-\dfrac{q\kappa}{r}\right)-\frac{\ell^{2}}{M^{2}r^{2}}=\dfrac{2}{M}\left(E-\dfrac{q\kappa}{r}-\frac{\ell^{2}}{2Mr^{2}}\right),

(51)

i.e.,

\displaystyle\dot{r}=\sqrt{\dfrac{2}{M}\left(E-\dfrac{q\kappa}{r}-\frac{\ell^{2}}{2Mr^{2}}\right)},

(52)

i.e.,

\displaystyle{\rm d}r=\sqrt{\dfrac{2}{M}\left(E-\dfrac{q\kappa}{r}-\frac{\ell^{2}}{2Mr^{2}}\right)}\,{\rm d}t.

(53)

The energy
E
and the angular momentum
\ell
are conserved. From Eq. (
49
) we have

{\rm d}\theta=\frac{\ell}{Mr^{2}}{\rm d}t.

(54)

Then
we attain

\frac{{\rm d}r}{\sqrt{\dfrac{2}{M}\left(E-\dfrac{q\kappa}{r}-\dfrac{\ell^{2}}{2Mr^{2}}\right)}}=\dfrac{Mr^{2}}{\ell}{\rm d}\theta,

(55)

i.e.,

\frac{\ell}{Mr^{2}}\frac{{\rm d}r}{\sqrt{\dfrac{2}{M}\left(E-\dfrac{q\kappa}{r}-\dfrac{\ell^{2}}{2Mr^{2}}\right)}}={\rm d}\theta,

(56)

We designate
r=\dfrac{1}{u}
, then

\displaystyle\dfrac{{\rm d}r}{{\rm d}\theta}=\dfrac{{\rm d}r}{{\rm d}u}\dfrac{{\rm d}u}{{\rm d}\theta}=-\dfrac{1}{u^{2}}\dfrac{{\rm d}u}{{\rm d}\theta},

(57)

thus

\frac{\ell}{Mr^{2}}\left[-\dfrac{1}{u^{2}}\right]\frac{{\rm d}u}{\sqrt{\dfrac{2}{M}\left(E-\dfrac{q\kappa}{r}-\dfrac{\ell^{2}}{2Mr^{2}}\right)}}={\rm d}\theta,

(58)

i.e.,

\frac{\ell u^{2}}{M}\left[-\dfrac{1}{u^{2}}\right]\frac{{\rm d}u}{\sqrt{\dfrac{2}{M}\left(E-{q\kappa}{u}-\dfrac{\ell^{2}u^{2}}{2M}\right)}}={\rm d}\theta,

(59)

i.e.,

-\frac{{\rm d}u}{\sqrt{\dfrac{2M}{\ell^{2}}\left(E-{q\kappa}{u}-\dfrac{\ell^{2}u^{2}}{2M}\right)}}={\rm d}\theta,

(60)

i.e.,

-\frac{{\rm d}u}{\sqrt{\dfrac{2M}{\ell^{2}}E-\dfrac{2Mq\kappa}{\ell^{2}}u-u^{2}}}={\rm d}\theta,

(61)

i.e.,

-\frac{{\rm d}u}{\sqrt{\dfrac{2M}{\ell^{2}}E+\left(\dfrac{Mq\kappa}{\ell^{2}}\right)^{2}-\left(u{+}\dfrac{Mq\kappa}{\ell^{2}}\right)^{2}}}={\rm d}\theta,

(62)

i.e.,

-\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{\dfrac{2M}{\ell^{2}}E+\left(\dfrac{Mq\kappa}{\ell^{2}}\right)^{2}}}\frac{{\rm d}\left(u{+}\dfrac{Mq\kappa}{\ell^{2}}\right)}{\sqrt{1-\left(\dfrac{u{+}\dfrac{Mq\kappa}{\ell^{2}}}{\sqrt{\dfrac{2M}{\ell^{2}}E+\left(\dfrac{Mq\kappa}{\ell^{2}}\right)^{2}}}\right)^{2}}}={\rm d}\theta.

(63)

Because

\dfrac{{\rm d}\arccos{x}}{{\rm d}x}=-\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{1-x^{2}}},

(64)

then we have

\arccos{\left(\frac{u{+}\dfrac{Mq\kappa}{\ell^{2}}}{\sqrt{\dfrac{2M}{\ell^{2}}E+\left(\dfrac{Mq\kappa}{\ell^{2}}\right)^{2}}}\right)}=\theta-\theta_{0},

(65)

i.e.,

u{+}\dfrac{Mq\kappa}{\ell^{2}}={\sqrt{\dfrac{2M}{\ell^{2}}E+\left(\dfrac{Mq\kappa}{\ell^{2}}\right)^{2}}}\,\cos\left(\theta-\theta_{0}\right),

(66)

i.e.,

u={-}\dfrac{Mq\kappa}{\ell^{2}}+{\sqrt{\dfrac{2M}{\ell^{2}}E+\left(\dfrac{Mq\kappa}{\ell^{2}}\right)^{2}}}\,\cos\left(\theta-\theta_{0}\right),

(67)

i.e.,

r=\frac{1}{\dfrac{M{|q\kappa|}}{\ell^{2}}+{\sqrt{\dfrac{2M}{\ell^{2}}E+\left(\dfrac{Mq\kappa}{\ell^{2}}\right)^{2}}}\,\cos\left(\theta-\theta_{0}\right)},

(68)

i.e.,

r=\dfrac{\ell^{2}}{M{|q\kappa|}}\frac{1}{1+\dfrac{\sqrt{\dfrac{2M}{\ell^{2}}E+\left(\dfrac{Mq\kappa}{\ell^{2}}\right)^{2}}}{\dfrac{M{|q\kappa|}}{\ell^{2}}}\,\cos\left(\theta-\theta_{0}\right)}.

(69)

By comparing Eq. (
46
) and Eq. (
69
), we have the eccentricity ratio as

\displaystyle{\rm e}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{\sqrt{\dfrac{2M}{\ell^{2}}E+\left(\dfrac{Mq\kappa}{\ell^{2}}\right)^{2}}}{\dfrac{M{|q\kappa|}}{\ell^{2}}}=\sqrt{1+\dfrac{\dfrac{2M}{\ell^{2}}E}{\left(\dfrac{Mq\kappa}{\ell^{2}}\right)^{2}}}=\sqrt{1+\dfrac{2\ell^{2}}{M(q\kappa)^{2}}E},

(70)

which implies that the value of
{\rm e}
is determined by the energy
E
.

\blacksquare

Remark 2
.

Here, we would like to study again the relation between the eccentricity ratio
{\rm e}
and the energy
E
based on the following formula

\displaystyle r=\dfrac{\ell^{2}}{M|q\kappa|}\dfrac{1}{\left[1+{\rm e}\cos(\theta-\theta_{0})\right]}.

(71)

Still, we restrict to the case of
q\kappa<0
(i.e.,
\vec{\mathbb{F}}
is an attractive force).

From Eq. (
50
) we have

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}M\left(\dot{r}^{2}+\frac{\ell^{2}}{M^{2}r^{2}}\right)+\dfrac{q\kappa}{r}.

(72)

Based on Eq. (
71
) one has

\displaystyle\dot{r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{{\rm d}r}{{\rm d}t}=\dfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\left[\dfrac{\ell^{2}}{M|q\kappa|}\dfrac{1}{\left[1+{\rm e}\cos(\theta-\theta_{0})\right]}\right]=\dfrac{\ell^{2}}{M|q\kappa|}\dfrac{{\rm e}\sin(\theta-\theta_{0})}{\left[1+{\rm e}\cos(\theta-\theta_{0})\right]^{2}}\dfrac{{\rm d}\theta}{{\rm d}t}

(73)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{\ell^{2}}{M|q\kappa|}\dfrac{{\rm e}\sin(\theta-\theta_{0})}{\left[1+{\rm e}\cos(\theta-\theta_{0})\right]^{2}}\dot{\theta}=\dfrac{\ell^{2}}{M|q\kappa|}\dfrac{{\rm e}\sin(\theta-\theta_{0})}{\left[1+{\rm e}\cos(\theta-\theta_{0})\right]^{2}}\dfrac{\ell}{mr^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{\ell^{3}}{M^{2}|q\kappa|}\dfrac{{\rm e}\sin(\theta-\theta_{0})}{r^{2}\left[1+{\rm e}\cos(\theta-\theta_{0})\right]^{2}}=\dfrac{\ell^{3}}{M^{2}|q\kappa|}{\rm e}\sin(\theta-\theta_{0})\left(\dfrac{M|q\kappa|}{\ell^{2}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{|q\kappa|}{\ell}{\rm e}\sin(\theta-\theta_{0}).

Then the energy becomes

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}M\left(\dot{r}^{2}+\frac{\ell^{2}}{M^{2}r^{2}}\right)+\dfrac{q\kappa}{r}

(74)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}M\left[\dfrac{|q\kappa|}{\ell}{\rm e}\sin(\theta-\theta_{0})\right]^{2}+\dfrac{\ell^{2}}{2M}\left[\dfrac{M|q\kappa|}{\ell^{2}}\left[1+{\rm e}\cos(\theta-\theta_{0})\right]\right]^{2}+q\kappa\left[\dfrac{M|q\kappa|}{\ell^{2}}\left[1+{\rm e}\cos(\theta-\theta_{0})\right]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{M|q\kappa|^{2}}{2\ell^{2}}{\rm e}^{2}\sin^{2}(\theta-\theta_{0})+\frac{M|q\kappa|^{2}}{2\ell^{2}}\left[1+{\rm e}\cos(\theta-\theta_{0})\right]^{2}+\frac{M|q\kappa|q\kappa}{\ell^{2}}\left[1+{\rm e}\cos(\theta-\theta_{0})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{M|q\kappa|^{2}}{2\ell^{2}}{\rm e}^{2}\sin^{2}(\theta-\theta_{0})+\frac{M|q\kappa|^{2}}{2\ell^{2}}\left[1+{\rm e}\cos(\theta-\theta_{0})\right]^{2}-\frac{M|q\kappa|^{2}}{\ell^{2}}\left[1+{\rm e}\cos(\theta-\theta_{0})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{M|q\kappa|^{2}}{2\ell^{2}}\left\{{\rm e}^{2}\sin^{2}(\theta-\theta_{0})+\left[1+{\rm e}\cos(\theta-\theta_{0})\right]^{2}-2\left[1+{\rm e}\cos(\theta-\theta_{0})\right]\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{M|q\kappa|^{2}}{2\ell^{2}}\left\{{\rm e}^{2}\sin^{2}(\theta-\theta_{0})+\left[1+{\rm e}\cos(\theta-\theta_{0})-1\right]^{2}-1\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{M|q\kappa|^{2}}{2\ell^{2}}\left\{{\rm e}^{2}\sin^{2}(\theta-\theta_{0})+{\rm e}^{2}\cos^{2}(\theta-\theta_{0})-1\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{M|q\kappa|^{2}}{2\ell^{2}}({\rm e}^{2}-1),

thus

\displaystyle{\rm e}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{1+\dfrac{2\ell^{2}}{M|q\kappa|^{2}}E},

(75)

which recovers Eq. (
70
).
\blacksquare

Remark 3
.

Classically, for the attractive force (i.e.,
q\kappa<0
), the kinetic energy
\frac{1}{2}mv^{2}\geq 0

and the potential energy
\dfrac{q\kappa}{r}<0
, thus the total energy
E
can be less than, or equal to,
or greater than 0. However, for the case of repulsive force
\vec{\mathbb{F}}
, one has
q\kappa>0
.
Because both kinetic energy and potential energy are greater than 0, namely,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}mv^{2}\geq 0,\;\;\;\;\;\dfrac{q\kappa}{r}>0,

(76)

it is easy to prove the energy of the physical system

\displaystyle E=\frac{1}{2}mv^{2}+\dfrac{q\kappa}{r}>0,

(77)

thus the eccentricity ratio
{\rm e}>1
. This means that the trajectory of particle 1 is a hyperbola
if
\vec{\mathbb{F}}
is repulsive, thus particle 1 can escape the constraint of particle 2, and particles 1
and 2 cannot form a stable physical system.
\blacksquare

In conclusion, when
\vec{\mathbb{F}}
is an attractive force and the energy
E<0
, one has the eccentricity
ratio
0\leq{\rm e}<1
. Then particle 1 moves around particle 2, and its trajectory is an ellipse (or a circle
when
{\rm e}=0
). Thus, the attractive Coulomb-type force
\vec{\mathbb{F}}
serves as a fundamental force to
bind two charged particles. In this way, it is a good reason to explain why an electron and a proton can form a
stable hydrogen atom. This is the classical picture of binding two particles with Coulomb potential.

III.2
Quantum Picture: Binding Two Particles with Coulomb Potential

In quantum mechanics, the position operator
\vec{r}
and the linear momentum operator
\vec{p}
are not commutative, they satisfy
the following basic commutation relation

\displaystyle[r_{\alpha},p_{\beta}]=\mathrm{i}\hbar\delta_{\alpha\beta},

(78)

with
\delta_{\alpha\beta}
being the Kronecker delta function, and
\hbar
being the Planck constant. Explicitly, one has

\displaystyle[x,p_{x}]=\mathrm{i}\hbar,\;\;[y,p_{y}]=\mathrm{i}\hbar,\;\;[z,p_{z}]=\mathrm{i}\hbar,

\displaystyle[x,p_{y}]=[x,p_{z}]=0,\;\;\;[y,p_{x}]=[y,p_{z}]=0,\;\;\;[z,p_{x}]=[z,p_{y}]=0.

(79)

Physically, it means that the position and the momentum of a particle cannot be measured simultaneously, thus the
concept of “force” becomes a bit out of place. To avoid such an embarrassment, scientists have used the term “interaction” to
replace the term “force”, i.e., “fundamental force” turns to “fundamental interaction”.

In classical picture, scientists adopted “force” (such as the Coulomb force) to describe the interaction between two particles.
However, in quantum picture, scientists adopted “potential” (such as the Coulomb potential) to describe the interaction between
two particles. In quantum mechanics, when a two-body system can have bounded states, then these two particles form a stable physical
system, such as the well-known hydrogen atom.

In the following, we provide an example of a hydrogen atom, where particle 1 is the electron, and particle 2 is the proton.
The Schrödinger-type Hamiltonian of the physical system is given by

\mathcal{H}=\frac{\vec{p}^{\,2}}{2M}+V(r)=\frac{\vec{p}^{\,2}}{2M}+\frac{q\kappa}{r},

(80)

with
q=-e
,
\kappa=Q=Ze
, and
Z=1
. The eigen-problem reads

\mathcal{H}\,\Psi(\vec{r})=E\,\Psi(\vec{r}),

(81)

where
E
is the energy and
\Psi(\vec{r})
is the wavefunction.
Because

\displaystyle\vec{p}^{\;2}=-\hbar^{2}\dfrac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\,r^{2}}-2\hbar^{2}\dfrac{1}{r}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\,r}+\dfrac{\vec{\ell}^{\;2}}{r^{2}},

(82)

where

\displaystyle\vec{\ell}=\vec{r}\times\vec{p}

(83)

is the orbital angular momentum operator, then we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{2M}\left(-\hbar^{2}\dfrac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\,r^{2}}-2\hbar^{2}\dfrac{1}{r}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\,r}+\dfrac{\vec{\ell}^{\;2}}{r^{2}}\right)+\frac{q\kappa}{r}.

(84)

It can be proved that

\displaystyle[\mathcal{H},\vec{\ell}^{\,2}]=0,\;\;\;\;\;[\mathcal{H},\ell_{z}]=0,\;\;\;\;\;[\vec{\ell}^{\,2},\ell_{z}]=0,

(85)

thus
\{H,\vec{\ell}^{\,2},\ell_{z}\}
forms a common set to solve the eigen-problem. Here
\ell_{z}
is the
z
-component of
\vec{\ell}
.

Let us separate the wavefunction into the radial part and the angular part as follows

\Psi(\vec{r})=R(r)\,Y_{lm}(\theta,\phi),

(86)

which satisfies

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}\,R(r)\,Y_{lm}(\theta,\phi)=E\,R(r)\,Y_{lm}(\theta,\phi),

\displaystyle\vec{\ell}^{\,2}\,R(r)\,Y_{lm}(\theta,\phi)=l(l+1)\hbar^{2}\,R(r)\,Y_{lm}(\theta,\phi),

\displaystyle{\ell}_{z}\,R(r)\,Y_{lm}(\theta,\phi)=m\hbar\,R(r)\,Y_{lm}(\theta,\phi),

(87)

or

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}\,R(r)=E\,R(r),

\displaystyle\vec{\ell}^{\,2}\,Y_{lm}(\theta,\phi)=l(l+1)\hbar^{2}\,Y_{lm}(\theta,\phi),

\displaystyle{\ell}_{z}\,Y_{lm}(\theta,\phi)=m\hbar\,Y_{lm}(\theta,\phi),

(88)

where the angular-part wavefunction
Y_{lm}(\theta,\phi)
is the well-known
spherical harmonics function
.

Remark 4
.

The Eigen-Problem of
\{\vec{\ell}^{\;2},\ell_{z}\}
.
In the spherical coordinate
(r,\theta,\phi)
, the
orbit angular momentum operator

\vec{\ell}=(\ell_{x},\ell_{y},\ell_{z})
is given by

\displaystyle\ell_{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm i}\hbar\left(\sin\phi\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\,\theta}+\cos\phi\cot\theta\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\,\phi}\right),

\displaystyle\ell_{y}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm i}\hbar\left(-\cos\phi\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\,\theta}+\sin\phi\cot\theta\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\,\phi}\right),

\displaystyle\ell_{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-{\rm i}\hbar\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\,\phi},

(89)

and its squared operator is given by

\displaystyle\vec{\ell}^{\;2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\hbar^{2}\Biggl[\dfrac{1}{\sin\theta}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\,\theta}\left(\sin\theta\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\,\theta}\right)+\dfrac{1}{{\sin^{2}}\theta}\dfrac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\,\phi^{2}}\Biggr],

(90)

or

\displaystyle\vec{\ell}^{\;2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\hbar^{2}\left[\dfrac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\theta^{2}}+\cot\theta\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta}+(1+\cot^{2}\theta)\dfrac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\phi^{2}}\right].

(91)

Since
[\vec{\ell}^{\;2},\ell_{z}]=0
, one usually solves the eigen-problem of

\vec{\ell}^{\;2}
in the common set
\{\vec{\ell}^{\;2},\ell_{z}\}
, viz.

\displaystyle\vec{\ell}^{\;2}\;Y_{lm}(\theta,\phi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle l(l+1)\hbar^{2}\;Y_{lm}(\theta,\phi),

\displaystyle\ell_{z}\;Y_{lm}(\theta,\phi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m\hbar\;Y_{lm}(\theta,\phi),

(92)

with quantum numbers
l=0,1,2,\cdots
, and
m=0,\pm 1,\pm 2,\cdots,\pm l
. When
m>0
, the spherical harmonics function reads

\displaystyle Y_{lm}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{2l+1}{4\pi}\frac{(l-|m|)!}{(l+|m|)!}}P^{|m|}_{l}(\cos\theta)e^{{\rm i}m\phi},

(93)

and when
m<0
, it reads

\displaystyle Y_{lm}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{m}\sqrt{\frac{2l+1}{4\pi}\frac{(l-|m|)!}{(l+|m|)!}}P^{|m|}_{l}(\cos\theta)e^{{\rm i}m\phi}.

(94)

The function
P_{l}^{m}(\cos\theta)
satisfies

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{\sin\theta}\dfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\,\theta}\left[\sin\theta\dfrac{{\rm d}\,P_{l}^{m}(\cos\theta)}{{\rm d}\,\theta}\right]+\left[l(l+1)-\dfrac{m^{2}}{{\sin^{2}}\theta}\right]P_{l}^{m}(\cos\theta)=0,

(95)

and

\displaystyle P_{l}^{m}(z)=(-1)^{m}(1-z^{2})^{m/2}\frac{{\rm d}^{m}P_{l}(z)}{{\rm d}z^{m}}.

(96)

\blacksquare

Next, we need to determine the radial-part wavefunction
R(r)
, which satisfies

\displaystyle\left[\dfrac{1}{2M}\left(-\hbar^{2}\dfrac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\,r^{2}}-2\hbar^{2}\dfrac{1}{r}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\,r}+\dfrac{l(l+1)\hbar^{2}}{r^{2}}\right)+\frac{q\kappa}{r}\right]R(r)=E\,R(r),

(97)

or

\displaystyle\left[\dfrac{1}{2M}\left(-\hbar^{2}\dfrac{{\rm d}^{2}}{{\rm d}r^{2}}-2\hbar^{2}\dfrac{1}{r}\dfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}r}+\dfrac{l(l+1)\hbar^{2}}{r^{2}}\right)-\frac{|q\kappa|}{r}\right]R(r)=E\,R(r),

(98)

for
q\kappa<0
(which corresponds to the attractive potential).

Let us solve Eq. (
98
) by considering three different situations:
E<0
,
E=0
, and
E>0
. The conclusion
is: (i) For
E<0
, the two-particle system can have physically admissible wavefunctions; (ii) For
E=0
and
E>0
, the
two-particle system cannot have physically admissible wavefunctions (i.e, the wavefuncions
\Psi(\vec{r})
are divergent
and cannot be normalized), thus these cases are excluded.

In the following, we focus on the eigen-problem with
E<0
.

III.2.1
The Eigen-Problem with
E<0

Let us analyze the asymptotic behaviors of
R(r)
.

Case (i).—
When
r\rightarrow 0
, Eq. (
98
)
becomes

\displaystyle\dfrac{{\rm d}^{2}R(r)}{{\rm d}r^{2}}+\dfrac{2}{r}\dfrac{{\rm d}R(r)}{{\rm d}r}-\dfrac{l(l+1)}{r^{2}}R(r)=0.

(99)

In the region near to
r=0
, let
R(r)\propto r^{\nu}
, after substituting it into above equation, we have

\displaystyle\nu(\nu-1)r^{\nu-2}+2\nu r^{\nu-2}-l(l+1)r^{\nu-2}=0,

(100)

which leads to

\displaystyle\nu(\nu+1)-l(l+1)=0.

(101)

Based on which, one obtains

\displaystyle\nu_{1}=l,\;\;\;{\rm or}\;\;\;\nu_{2}=-(l+1).

(102)

To avoid
\lim\limits_{r\rightarrow 0}R(r)\rightarrow\infty
, from the
viewpoint of physics, we choose
\nu=\nu_{1}=l\geq 0
. When
l=0
, we specially have
\nu=0
.

Case (ii).—
When
r\rightarrow\infty
, Eq.
(
98
) becomes

\displaystyle\dfrac{{\rm d}^{2}R(r)}{{\rm d}\,r^{2}}+\epsilon\,R(r)=0,

(103)

with

\displaystyle\epsilon=\dfrac{2ME}{\hbar^{2}}<0.

(104)

Then we attain

\displaystyle R(r)\propto{\rm e}^{\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r},\;\;\;{\rm or}\;\;\;R(r)\propto{\rm e}^{-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r}.

(105)

To avoid
\lim\limits_{r\rightarrow\infty}R(r)\rightarrow\infty
, we
choose the solution
R(r)\propto{\rm e}^{-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r}
.

Based on the above analysis, we may set
R(r)
as

\displaystyle R(r)=r^{\nu}{\rm e}^{-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r}F(r),

(106)

with
\nu=l
. After that, we can calculate

\displaystyle\dfrac{{\rm d}\,R(r)}{{\rm d}\,r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nu\,r^{\nu-1}{\rm e}^{-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r}F(r)-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r^{\nu}{\rm e}^{-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r}\,F(r)+r^{\nu}{\rm e}^{-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r}\,\dfrac{{\rm d}\,F(r)}{{\rm d}\,r}

(107)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r^{\nu}{\rm e}^{-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r}\Biggr[\biggr(\dfrac{\nu}{r}-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\biggr)F(r)+\dfrac{{\rm d}F(r)}{{\rm d}r}\Biggr]=r^{\nu}{\rm e}^{-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r}\,C(r),

thus

\displaystyle\dfrac{{\rm d}^{2}\,R(r)}{{\rm d}\,r^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{{\rm d}^{2}\,\Bigl[r^{\nu}{\rm e}^{-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r}\,C(r)\Bigr]}{{\rm d}\,r^{2}}

(108)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r^{\nu}{\rm e}^{-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r}\Biggr[\biggr(\dfrac{s}{r}-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\biggr)C(r)+\dfrac{{\rm d}\,C(r)}{{\rm d}\,r}\Biggr]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r^{\nu}{\rm e}^{-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r}\Biggl\{\biggr(\dfrac{\nu}{r}-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\biggr)\biggr[\biggr(\dfrac{\nu}{r}-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\biggr)F(r)+\dfrac{{\rm d}F(r)}{{\rm d}r}\biggr]+\dfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\,r}\,\biggr[\biggr(\dfrac{\nu}{r}-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\biggr)F(r)+\dfrac{{\rm d}F(r)}{{\rm d}r}\biggr]\Biggr\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r^{\nu}{\rm e}^{-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r}\Biggl\{\biggr(\dfrac{\nu}{r}-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\biggr)\biggr[\biggr(\dfrac{\nu}{r}-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\biggr)F(r)+\dfrac{{\rm d}F(r)}{{\rm d}r}\biggr]+\biggr[-\dfrac{\nu}{r^{2}}F(r)+\biggr(\dfrac{\nu}{r}-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\biggr)\dfrac{{\rm d}F(r)}{{\rm d}r}+\dfrac{{\rm d}^{2}F(r)}{{\rm d}\,r^{2}}\biggr]\Biggr\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r^{\nu}{\rm e}^{-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r}\Biggl\{\dfrac{{\rm d}^{2}F(r)}{{\rm d}\,r^{2}}+2\biggr(\dfrac{\nu}{r}-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\biggr)\dfrac{{\rm d}F(r)}{{\rm d}r}+\biggl[\Bigl(\dfrac{\nu}{r}-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\Bigr)^{2}-\dfrac{\nu}{r^{2}}\biggr]F(r)\Biggr\}.

Then,

\displaystyle\dfrac{{\rm d}^{2}\,R(r)}{{\rm d}\,r^{2}}+\dfrac{2}{r}\dfrac{{\rm d}\,R(r)}{{\rm d}\,r}-\dfrac{l(l+1)}{r^{2}}R(r)-\dfrac{2Mq\kappa}{\hbar^{2}r}R(r)+\epsilon R(r)

(109)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r^{\nu}{\rm e}^{-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r}\Biggl\{\dfrac{{\rm d}^{2}F(r)}{{\rm d}\,r^{2}}+2\biggr(\dfrac{\nu}{r}-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\biggr)\dfrac{{\rm d}F(r)}{{\rm d}r}+\biggl[\Bigl(\dfrac{\nu}{r}-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\Bigr)^{2}-\dfrac{\nu}{r^{2}}\biggr]F(r)\Biggr\}

\displaystyle+\dfrac{2}{r}r^{\nu}{\rm e}^{-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r}\Biggr[\biggr(\dfrac{\nu}{r}-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\biggr)F(r)+\dfrac{{\rm d}F(r)}{{\rm d}r}\Biggr]+\left(\epsilon-\dfrac{2Mq\kappa}{\hbar^{2}r}-\dfrac{l(l+1)}{r^{2}}\right)r^{\nu}{\rm e}^{-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r}F(r)=0,

i.e.,

\displaystyle\Biggl\{\dfrac{{\rm d}^{2}F(r)}{{\rm d}\,r^{2}}+2\biggr(\dfrac{\nu}{r}-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\biggr)\dfrac{{\rm d}F(r)}{{\rm d}r}+\biggl[\Bigl(\dfrac{\nu}{r}-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\Bigr)^{2}-\dfrac{\nu}{r^{2}}\biggr]F(r)\Biggr\}

\displaystyle+\dfrac{2}{r}\Biggr[\biggr(\dfrac{\nu}{r}-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\biggr)F(r)+\dfrac{{\rm d}F(r)}{{\rm d}r}\Biggr]+\left(\epsilon-\dfrac{2Mq\kappa}{\hbar^{2}r}-\dfrac{l(l+1)}{r^{2}}\right)F(r)=0,

(110)

i.e.,

\displaystyle\dfrac{{\rm d}^{2}F(r)}{{\rm d}\,r^{2}}+\left[2\biggr(\dfrac{\nu}{r}-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\biggr)+\dfrac{2}{r}\right]\dfrac{{\rm d}F(r)}{{\rm d}r}

\displaystyle+\left\{\biggl[\Bigl(\dfrac{\nu}{r}-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\Bigr)^{2}-\dfrac{\nu}{r^{2}}\biggr]+\dfrac{2}{r}\Biggr[\biggr(\dfrac{\nu}{r}-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\biggr)\Biggr]+\left(\epsilon-\dfrac{2Mq\kappa}{\hbar^{2}r}-\dfrac{l(l+1)}{r^{2}}\right)\right\}F(r)=0,

(111)

i.e.,

\displaystyle\dfrac{{\rm d}^{2}F(r)}{{\rm d}\,r^{2}}+\biggr[\dfrac{2(\nu+1)}{r}-2\sqrt{-\epsilon}\biggr]\dfrac{{\rm d}\,F(r)}{{\rm d}\,r}+\biggr[\dfrac{\nu(\nu+1)-l(l+1)}{r^{2}}-\dfrac{2(\nu+1)\sqrt{-\epsilon}+\dfrac{2Mq\kappa}{\hbar^{2}}}{r}\biggr]F(r)=0,

(112)

i.e.,

\displaystyle\dfrac{{\rm d}^{2}F(r)}{{\rm d}\,r^{2}}+\biggr[\dfrac{2(\nu+1)}{r}-2\sqrt{-\epsilon}\biggr]\dfrac{{\rm d}\,F(r)}{{\rm d}\,r}+\biggr[-\dfrac{2(\nu+1)\sqrt{-\epsilon}+\dfrac{2Mq\kappa}{\hbar^{2}}}{r}\biggr]F(r)=0,

(113)

i.e.,

\displaystyle r\dfrac{{\rm d}^{2}F(r)}{{\rm d}\,r^{2}}+\biggr[2(\nu+1)-2\sqrt{-\epsilon}\;r\biggr]\dfrac{{\rm d}\,F(r)}{{\rm d}\,r}-\biggr[2(\nu+1)\sqrt{-\epsilon}+\dfrac{2Mq\kappa}{\hbar^{2}}\biggr]F(r)=0,

(114)

i.e.,

\displaystyle(\sqrt{-\epsilon})^{2}r\dfrac{{\rm d}^{2}F(z)}{{\rm d}(\sqrt{-\epsilon}\;r)^{2}}+\sqrt{-\epsilon}\;\biggr[2(\nu+1)-2\sqrt{-\epsilon}\;r\biggr]\dfrac{{\rm d}\,F(z)}{{\rm d}\,(\sqrt{-\epsilon}\;r)}-\biggr[2(\nu+1)\sqrt{-\epsilon}+\dfrac{2Mq\kappa}{\hbar^{2}}\biggr]F(z)=0.

(115)

Let

\displaystyle z=\sqrt{-\epsilon}\;r,

(116)

then Eq. (
115
) becomes

\displaystyle\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,z\dfrac{{\rm d}^{2}F(z)}{{\rm d}\,z^{2}}+\sqrt{-\epsilon}\;\biggr[2(\nu+1)-2z\biggr]\dfrac{{\rm d}\,F(z)}{{\rm d}\,z}-\biggr[2(\nu+1)\sqrt{-\epsilon}+\dfrac{2Mq\kappa}{\hbar^{2}}\biggr]F(z)=0,

(117)

i.e.,

\displaystyle z\dfrac{{\rm d}^{2}F(z)}{{\rm d}\,z^{2}}+\biggr[2(\nu+1)-2z\biggr]\dfrac{{\rm d}\,F(z)}{{\rm d}\,z}-\biggr[2(\nu+1)+\dfrac{2Mq\kappa}{\hbar^{2}\sqrt{-\epsilon}}\biggr]F(z)=0,

(118)

i.e.,

\displaystyle\tau\dfrac{{\rm d}^{2}F(\tau)}{{\rm d}\,\tau^{2}}+\biggr[2(\nu+1)-\tau\biggr]\dfrac{{\rm d}\,F(\tau)}{{\rm d}\,\tau}-\left[(\nu+1)+\dfrac{Mq\kappa}{\hbar^{2}\sqrt{-\epsilon}}\right]F(\tau)=0,

(119)

with
\tau=2\,z
.

Remarkably, let us set

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\nu+1)+\dfrac{Mq\kappa}{\hbar^{2}\sqrt{-\epsilon}}=(l+1)+\dfrac{Mq\kappa}{\hbar^{2}\sqrt{-\epsilon}},\;\;\;\;\;\;\gamma=2(\nu+1)=2(l+1),

(120)

then Eq.(
119
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\tau\dfrac{{\rm d}^{2}\,F(\tau)}{{\rm d}\,\tau^{2}}+(\gamma-\tau)\dfrac{{\rm d}\,F(\tau)}{{\rm d}\,\tau}-\alpha\,F(\tau)=0,

(121)

which is a confluent hypergeometric equation with general solution

\displaystyle F(\tau)=C_{1}\,F(\alpha;\,\gamma;\,\tau)+C_{2}\,\tau^{1-\gamma}\,F(\alpha+1-\gamma;\,2-\gamma;\,\tau),

(122)

where
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
are two constants, and

\displaystyle F(\alpha;\gamma;\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\dfrac{(\alpha)_{k}}{k!\,(\gamma)_{k}}\tau^{k}=1+\dfrac{\alpha}{\gamma}\tau+\dfrac{\alpha(\alpha+1)}{2!\,\gamma(\gamma+1)}\tau^{2}+\dfrac{\alpha(\alpha+1)(\alpha+2)}{3!\,\gamma(\gamma+1)(\gamma+2)}\tau^{3}+\cdots

(123)

is the confluent hypergeometric function, including

\displaystyle(\alpha)_{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha(\alpha+1)\cdots(\alpha+k-1),\;\;\;\;\;(\gamma)_{k}=\gamma(\gamma+1)\cdots(\gamma+k-1).

(124)

For convenience, one can denote the two hypergeometric functions
as

\displaystyle w_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle F(\alpha;\gamma;\tau)=F\left((l+1)+\dfrac{Mq\kappa}{\hbar^{2}\sqrt{-\epsilon}};\,2(l+1);\,2\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r\right),

(125)

\displaystyle w_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau^{1-\gamma}F(\alpha-\gamma+1;\,2-\gamma;\,\tau)=\left(2\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r\right)^{1-\gamma}F(\alpha-\gamma+1;\,2-\gamma;\,2\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r),

(126)

namely

\displaystyle F(r)=C_{1}\,w_{1}+C_{2}\,w_{2}.

(127)

By substituting Eq. (
125
) and Eq. (
126
) into Eq. (
106
), one then
obtains the radial function as

\displaystyle R(r)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r^{\nu}{\rm e}^{-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r}F(r)=r^{\nu}{\rm e}^{-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r}(C_{1}\,w_{1}+C_{2}\,w_{2})=C_{1}R_{1}(r)+C_{2}R_{2}(r).

(128)

Let us study
R_{2}(r)
. We need to check the behavior of
R_{2}(r)
when
r\rightarrow 0
, which is

\displaystyle\lim_{r\rightarrow 0}R_{2}(r)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{r\rightarrow 0}r^{\nu}{\rm e}^{-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r}\left[\left(2\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r\right)^{1-\gamma}F(\alpha-\gamma+1;\,2-\gamma;\,2\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r)\right]

(129)

\displaystyle\propto

\displaystyle\lim_{r\rightarrow 0}r^{\nu}{\rm e}^{-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r}\left[\left(2\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r\right)^{1-\gamma}\right]

\displaystyle\propto

\displaystyle\lim_{r\rightarrow 0}\left[r^{l}r^{1-\gamma}\right]=\lim_{r\rightarrow 0}\left[r^{l+1-\gamma}\right]=\lim_{r\rightarrow 0}r^{-(l+1)}\rightarrow\infty,

thus
R_{2}(r)
is not a physically admissible solution.

Let us study
R_{1}(r)
. On one hand, to avoid
\lim\limits_{r\rightarrow\infty}R_{1}(r)\rightarrow\infty
, the function
w_{1}
has to be a truncated polynomial, i.e.,

\displaystyle\alpha=(l+1)+\dfrac{Mq\kappa}{\hbar^{2}\sqrt{-\epsilon}}=-N,\;\;\;\ (N=0,1,2,...).

(130)

[Note: One may easily observe that for the repulsive potential (i.e.,
q\kappa>0
), Eq. (
130
) is not valid.]

On the other hand, we need to check the behavior of
R_{1}(r)
when
r\rightarrow 0
, which is

\displaystyle\lim_{r\rightarrow 0}R_{1}(r)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{r\rightarrow 0}r^{\nu}{\rm e}^{-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r}\left[F(\alpha;\gamma;\tau)\right]=\lim_{r\rightarrow 0}r^{\nu}{\rm e}^{-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r}=\lim_{r\rightarrow 0}r^{s}=\lim_{r\rightarrow 0}r^{l}=0,\;\;{\rm or}\;\;1,

(131)

thus
R_{1}(r)
is the physical solution.

Based on Eq. (
130
). we have

\displaystyle\dfrac{Mq\kappa}{\hbar^{2}\sqrt{-\epsilon}}=-N-(l+1),

(132)

i.e.,

\displaystyle-\dfrac{M|q\kappa|}{\hbar^{2}\sqrt{-\epsilon}}=-N-(l+1),

(133)

i.e.,

\displaystyle\dfrac{M|q\kappa|}{\hbar^{2}\sqrt{-\epsilon}}=N+(l+1).

(134)

Let us denote the principle quantum number as

\displaystyle n=N+(l+1),

(135)

then we have

\displaystyle\dfrac{M|q\kappa|}{\hbar^{2}\sqrt{-\epsilon}}=n,\;\;(n=1,2,3,...)

(136)

i.e.,

\displaystyle\dfrac{\hbar^{2}\sqrt{-\epsilon}}{M|q\kappa|}=\dfrac{1}{n},

(137)

i.e.,

\displaystyle-\dfrac{\hbar^{4}\epsilon}{M^{2}(q\kappa)^{2}}=\dfrac{1}{n^{2}},

(138)

i.e.,

\displaystyle\dfrac{\hbar^{4}\dfrac{2ME}{\hbar^{2}}}{M^{2}(q\kappa)^{2}}=-\dfrac{1}{n^{2}},

(139)

i.e.,

\displaystyle E_{n}=-\dfrac{M(q\kappa)^{2}}{2\hbar^{2}}\dfrac{1}{n^{2}},\;\;(n=1,2,3,...),

(140)

or

\displaystyle E_{n}=-\dfrac{Z^{2}Me^{4}}{2\hbar^{2}}\dfrac{1}{n^{2}},\;\;(n=1,2,3,...),

(141)

which is just the energy of a hydrogen atom (
Z=1
).

III.2.2
The Expectation Value of
\vec{r}
under the Ground State

For the ground state of the hydrogen atom, the quantum numbers correspond to

\displaystyle n=1,\;\;\;l=0,\;\;\;m=0,

(142)

and

\displaystyle N=-\left[(l+1)+\dfrac{Mq\kappa}{\hbar^{2}\sqrt{-\epsilon}}\right]=0.

(143)

Then the angular-part wavefunction reads

\displaystyle Y_{00}(\theta,\phi)=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi}},\;\;\;\;\;\int_{0}^{\pi}|Y_{00}(\theta,\phi)|^{2}\sin\theta{{\rm d}\theta}\int_{0}^{2\pi}{\rm d}\phi=1,

(144)

and the radial-part wavefunction reads

\displaystyle R(r)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{1}r^{l}{\rm e}^{-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r}w_{1}=C_{1}r^{l}{\rm e}^{-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r}F\left((l+1)+\dfrac{Mq\kappa}{\hbar^{2}\sqrt{-\epsilon}};\,2(l+1);\,2\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r\right)

(145)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{1}r^{0}{\rm e}^{-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r}F\left(0;\,2;\,2\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r\right)=C_{1}{\rm e}^{-\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r},

with

\displaystyle\epsilon=\dfrac{2ME}{\hbar^{2}}=-\dfrac{2M}{\hbar^{2}}\dfrac{Z^{2}Me^{4}}{2\hbar^{2}}\dfrac{1}{n^{2}}=-\dfrac{M^{2}e^{4}}{\hbar^{4}}\dfrac{Z^{2}}{n^{2}}=-\dfrac{M^{2}e^{4}}{\hbar^{4}},

(146)

where
Z=n=1
. Due to

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{+\infty}r^{k}e^{-\mu r}{\rm d}r=k!\mu^{-k-1},

(147)

the normalized constant
C_{1}
is determined by

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{+\infty}|R(r)|^{2}r^{2}{\rm d}r\int_{0}^{\pi}|Y_{00}(\theta,\phi)|^{2}\sin\theta{{\rm d}\theta}\int_{0}^{2\pi}{\rm d}\phi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{+\infty}|R(r)|^{2}r^{2}{\rm d}r=C_{1}^{2}\int_{0}^{+\infty}r^{2}{\rm e}^{-2\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r}{\rm d}r

\displaystyle=C_{1}^{2}\,\left[2!\dfrac{1}{\left(2\sqrt{-\epsilon}\right)^{3}}\right]=\dfrac{C_{1}^{2}}{4\left(\sqrt{-\epsilon}\right)^{3}}=1,

(148)

i.e.,

\displaystyle C_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{4\left(\sqrt{-\epsilon}\right)^{3}}=\sqrt{4\left(\dfrac{Me^{2}}{\hbar^{2}}\right)^{3}}=\sqrt{\dfrac{4M^{3}e^{6}}{\hbar^{6}}}.

(149)

Furthermore, one may calculate the expectation values of
\{r,x,y,z\}
under the ground state, they are

\displaystyle\langle r\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{+\infty}|R(r)|^{2}r^{3}{\rm d}r\int_{0}^{\pi}|Y_{00}(\theta,\phi)|^{2}\sin\theta{{\rm d}\theta}\int_{0}^{2\pi}{\rm d}\phi=C_{1}^{2}\int_{0}^{+\infty}r^{3}{\rm e}^{-2\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r}{\rm d}r

(150)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{1}^{2}\left[3!\dfrac{1}{\left(2\sqrt{-\epsilon}\right)^{4}}\right]=4{\left(\sqrt{-\epsilon}\right)^{3}}\dfrac{6}{\left(2\sqrt{-\epsilon}\right)^{4}}=\dfrac{24}{16}\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{-\epsilon}}=\dfrac{3}{2}\sqrt{\dfrac{\hbar^{4}}{M^{2}e^{4}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3\hbar^{2}}{2Me^{2}}.

\displaystyle\langle x\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{+\infty}|R(r)|^{2}r^{3}{\rm d}r\int_{0}^{\pi}\sin^{2}\theta{{\rm d}\theta}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\cos\phi{\rm d}\phi=C_{1}^{2}\int_{0}^{+\infty}r^{3}{\rm e}^{-2\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r}{\rm d}r\int_{0}^{\pi}\sin^{2}\theta{{\rm d}\theta}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\cos\phi{\rm d}\phi=0

\displaystyle\langle y\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{+\infty}|R(r)|^{2}r^{3}{\rm d}r\int_{0}^{\pi}\sin^{2}\theta{{\rm d}\theta}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\sin\phi{\rm d}\phi=C_{1}^{2}\int_{0}^{+\infty}r^{3}{\rm e}^{-2\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r}{\rm d}r\int_{0}^{\pi}\sin^{2}\theta{{\rm d}\theta}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\sin\phi{\rm d}\phi=0,

\displaystyle\langle z\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{+\infty}|R(r)|^{2}r^{3}{\rm d}r\int_{0}^{\pi}\sin\theta\cos\theta{{\rm d}\theta}\int_{0}^{2\pi}{\rm d}\phi=C_{1}^{2}\int_{0}^{+\infty}r^{3}{\rm e}^{-2\sqrt{-\epsilon}\,r}{\rm d}r\int_{0}^{\pi}\sin\theta\cos\theta{{\rm d}\theta}\int_{0}^{2\pi}{\rm d}\phi=0.

(151)

This fact indicates that the probability distribution of the electron (i.e., particle 1) in space is spherically symmetric under the ground state.

IV
Spin-Dependent Coulomb-Type Potential: An Exact and Fundamental Solution of Yang-Mills Equations

Now, we face two facts in front of us: (i) The Coulomb-type potential is a simple but fundamental solution of Maxwell’s equations;
(ii) The Yang-Mills equations are the natural generalizations of Maxwell’s equations from the Abelian potentials to the non-Abelian ones.
Then it naturally gives rise to a scientific question: Can one derive some generalized Coulomb-type potentials based on Yang-Mills equations
(just like deriving the Coulomb-type potential from Maxwell’s equations)?
The answer is positive. We find that the spin-dependent Coulomb-type potential is an exact and fundamental solution of Yang-Mills equations.

IV.1
Spin Potentials
\{\vec{\mathcal{A}},\varphi\}
: An Exact and Fundamental Solution of Yang-Mills Equations

As mentioned before, there are two kinds of potentials: vector potential (denoted by
\vec{\mathcal{A}}
)
and scalar potential (denoted by
\varphi
). When
\vec{\mathcal{A}}
and
\varphi
contain the spin
operator
\vec{S}
, then we call
\vec{\mathcal{A}}
and
\varphi
as the spin vector potential and
the spin scalar potential, respectively. Or succinctly, we call
\{\vec{\mathcal{A}},\varphi\}
as a set of spin potentials.

Let
\vec{S}=(S_{x},S_{y},S_{z})
be the spin-
s
angular momentum operator (with the spin value
s=0,1/2,1,...
), which satisfies the following
commutation relations

\displaystyle[S_{x},S_{y}]=\mathrm{i}\hbar\;S_{z},\;\;\;[S_{y},S_{z}]=\mathrm{i}\hbar\;S_{x},\;\;\;[S_{z},S_{x}]=\mathrm{i}\hbar\;S_{y}.

(152)

or in the vector form as

\displaystyle\vec{S}\times\vec{S}=\mathrm{i}\hbar\;\vec{S}.

(153)

The square of
\vec{S}
satisfies

\displaystyle\vec{S}^{\,2}=s(s+1)\hbar^{2}.

(154)

Specially, when
s=1/2
, the spin operator
\vec{S}
can be realized by

\vec{S}=\frac{\hbar}{2}\vec{\sigma},

(155)

with
\vec{\sigma}=(\sigma_{x},\sigma_{y},\sigma_{z})
being the vector of Pauli matrices,
\vec{S}^{2}=\dfrac{3}{4}\hbar^{2}
, and

\displaystyle\sigma_{x}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\\
\end{array}\right),\;\;\;\sigma_{y}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&-{\rm i}\\
{\rm i}&0\\
\end{array}\right),\;\;\;\sigma_{z}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&-1\\
\end{array}\right).

We find that the set of spin potentials
\{\vec{\mathcal{A}},\varphi\}
can be an exact and fundamental solution of the Yang-Mills equation.
The result can be expressed in the following theorem.

Theorem 2
.

If the spin operator
\vec{S}
is considered, then Yang-Mills equations can have the following exact solutions for spin potentials

\displaystyle\vec{\mathcal{A}}=k\frac{\vec{r}\times\vec{S}}{r^{2}},

(163a)

\displaystyle\varphi=f_{1}(r)\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+f_{2}(r),

(163b)

where two functions
f_{1}(r)
and
f_{2}(r)
are given in Table
1
.

Table 1
:
Solutions of
f_{1}(r)
and
f_{2}(r)
. When
g=0
and
k\neq 0
, there are no solutions. One can set
\kappa_{4}=0
, for it is trivial.

(i)
g=0,k=0

(ii)
g=0,k\neq 0

(iii)
g\neq 0,k=0

(iv)
g\neq 0,k\neq 0

\;\;\;\;f_{1}(r)=\dfrac{\kappa_{1}}{r^{3}}+\kappa_{2}

No Solutions

f_{1}(r)=0

f_{1}(r)=\dfrac{\kappa_{1}}{r}

f_{2}(r)=\dfrac{\kappa_{3}}{r}+\kappa_{4}

f_{2}(r)=\dfrac{\kappa_{3}}{r}+\kappa_{4}

f_{2}(r)=\dfrac{\kappa_{3}}{r}+\kappa_{4}

g\hbar k=1

Proof.

In Ref.
chen_spin_2023
, the researchers have presented a kind of spin-dependent vector potentials to investigate the
spin Aharonov-Bohm effect. The form of the spin vector potential is given by

\vec{\mathcal{A}}=k\frac{\vec{r}\times\vec{S}}{r^{2}},

(164)

where
k
is a coefficient, and
\vec{S}
is the spin-
s
angular momentum operator.

Now the question becomes: For the fixed
\vec{\mathcal{A}}
in Eq. (
164
), then for what
\varphi
, the set of spin potentials

\{\vec{\mathcal{A}},\varphi\}
is an exact solution of Yang-Mills equations? To do so, let us determine the form of the
scalar potential
\varphi
first. For simplicity, let us consider the case of spin value
s=1/2
.

Because
\varphi
is (i) a scalar quantity; (ii) a function of
\vec{r}
and
\vec{S}
, then it is a possible combination of the following quantities

\displaystyle\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S},\;\;\;\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)^{2},\;\;\;\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)^{n},\;\;\;\left(\vec{r}\times\vec{S}\right)\cdot\vec{S},\;\;\;\vec{r}\cdot\left(\vec{S}\times\vec{S}\right),\;\;\;\left(\vec{S}\times\vec{S}\right)\cdot\vec{S}.

(165)

However, because

\displaystyle\left(\vec{r}\times\vec{S}\right)\cdot\vec{S}=\vec{r}\cdot\left(\vec{S}\times\vec{S}\right)={\rm i}\hbar\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right),

(166)

\displaystyle\vec{S}\cdot\left(\vec{r}\times\vec{S}\right)=-\vec{S}\cdot\left(\vec{S}\times\vec{r}\right)=-\left(\vec{S}\times\vec{S}\right)\cdot\vec{r}=-{\rm i}\hbar\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right),

(167)

\displaystyle\left(\vec{S}\times\vec{S}\right)\cdot\vec{S}={\rm i}\hbar S^{2}=\frac{{\rm i}3\hbar^{3}}{4},

(168)

\displaystyle\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)^{2}=\frac{r^{2}\hbar^{2}}{4},

(169)

thus
\varphi
has to be the following form

\varphi=f_{1}(r)\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+f_{2}(r),

(170)

where
f_{1}(r)
and
f_{2}(r)
are two functions of
r
, with
r=|\vec{r}|
.

Here we have chosen
\vec{\mathcal{A}}
and
\varphi
as the time-independent spin potentials, therefore

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial\vec{\mathcal{A}}}{\partial t}=0,\;\;\;\dfrac{\partial\varphi}{\partial t}=0,

(171)

which yields

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial\vec{\mathcal{E}}}{\partial t}=0,\;\;\;\dfrac{\partial\vec{\mathcal{B}}}{\partial t}=0,

(172)

i.e.,
\vec{\mathcal{E}}
and
\vec{\mathcal{B}}
are time-independent fields.

In the next, our task is to use Eq. (
11a
)-Eq. (
11d
) to determine two functions
f_{1}(r)
and
f_{2}(r)
.

Analysis 1: The study of Eq. (
11a
)—
We can have

\displaystyle\vec{\mathcal{E}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\dfrac{1}{c}\dfrac{\partial\,\vec{\mathcal{A}}}{\partial\,t}-\vec{\nabla}\varphi-{\rm i}\,g\left[\varphi,\ \vec{\mathcal{A}}\right]=-\vec{\nabla}\varphi-{\rm i}\,g\left[\varphi,\ \vec{\mathcal{A}}\right]

(173)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\vec{\nabla}\left[f_{1}(r)\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+f_{2}(r)\right]-{\rm i}\,g\left[f_{1}(r)\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+f_{2}(r),\ \vec{\mathcal{A}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\vec{\nabla}\left[f_{1}(r)\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)\right]-\vec{\nabla}\left[f_{2}(r)\right]-{\rm i}\,gf_{1}(r)\dfrac{k}{r^{2}}\left[\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right),\left(\vec{r}\times\vec{S}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\vec{\nabla}\left[f_{1}(r)\right]\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)-f_{1}(r)\vec{\nabla}\left[\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)\right]-\vec{\nabla}\left[f_{2}(r)\right]-{\rm i}\,gf_{1}(r)\dfrac{k}{r^{2}}\left[\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right),\left(\vec{r}\times\vec{S}\right)\right].

Because

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\left[f_{1}(r)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{\partial r}{\partial x}\;\hat{e}_{x}+\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{\partial r}{\partial y}\;\hat{e}_{y}+\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{\partial r}{\partial z}\;\hat{e}_{z}

(174)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{x}{r}\;\hat{e}_{x}+\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{y}{r}\;\hat{e}_{y}+\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{z}{r}\;\hat{e}_{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{\vec{r}}{r}},

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\left[f_{2}(r)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\dfrac{\partial f_{2}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{\vec{r}}{r}},

(175)

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\left[\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\left[xS_{x}+yS_{y}+zS_{z}\right]=S_{x}\;\hat{e}_{x}+S_{y}\;\hat{e}_{y}+S_{z}\;\hat{e}_{z}=\vec{S},

(176)

\displaystyle\left[\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right),\left(\vec{r}\times\vec{S}\right)_{z}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[xS_{x}+yS_{y}+zS_{z},xS_{y}-yS_{x}\right]=\left[xS_{x}+zS_{z},xS_{y}\right]-\left[yS_{y}+zS_{z},yS_{x}\right]

(177)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}\left[S_{x},S_{y}\right]+zx\left[S_{z},S_{y}\right]-y^{2}\left[S_{y},S_{x}\right]-zy\left[S_{z},S_{x}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}({\rm i}\hbar S_{z})+zx(-{\rm i}\hbar S_{x})-y^{2}(-{\rm i}\hbar S_{z})-zy({\rm i}\hbar S_{y})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm i}\hbar(x^{2}+y^{2})S_{z}-{\rm i}\hbar(xS_{x}+yS_{y})z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm i}\hbar r^{2}S_{z}-{\rm i}\hbar\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)z,

\displaystyle\left[\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right),\left(\vec{r}\times\vec{S}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm i}\hbar r^{2}\vec{S}-{\rm i}\hbar\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)\vec{r},

(178)

then we have the “electric” field as

\displaystyle\vec{\mathcal{E}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\vec{\nabla}\left[f_{1}(r)\right]\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)-f_{1}(r)\vec{\nabla}\left[\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)\right]-\vec{\nabla}\left[f_{2}(r)\right]-{\rm i}\,gf_{1}(r)\dfrac{k}{r^{2}}\left[\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right),\left(\vec{r}\times\vec{S}\right)\right]

(179)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{-}\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)\dfrac{\vec{r}}{r}-f_{1}(r)\vec{S}{-}\dfrac{\partial f_{2}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{\vec{r}}{r}-{\rm i}\,gf_{1}(r)\dfrac{k}{r^{2}}\left[{\rm i}\hbar r^{2}\vec{S}-{\rm i}\hbar\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)\vec{r}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{-}\left\{\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+\dfrac{\partial f_{2}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}+f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)\right\}\vec{r}+\left[-1+g\hbar k\right]f_{1}(r)\vec{S}.

Due to

\displaystyle\vec{r}\cdot\left(\vec{r}\times\vec{S}\right)=\left(\vec{r}\times\vec{S}\right)\cdot\vec{r}=0,

(180)

one easily has

\displaystyle{\rm i}\,g\Bigl(\vec{\mathcal{A}}\cdot\vec{\mathcal{E}}-\vec{\mathcal{E}}\cdot\vec{\mathcal{A}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm i}\,g\left[-1+g\hbar k\right]f_{1}(r)\left[\vec{\mathcal{A}}\cdot\vec{S}-\vec{S}\cdot\vec{\mathcal{A}}\right]

(181)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm i}\,g\left[-1+g\hbar k\right]f_{1}(r)\dfrac{k}{r^{2}}\left[\left(\vec{r}\times\vec{S}\right)\cdot\vec{S}-\vec{S}\cdot\left(\vec{r}\times\vec{S}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm i}\,g\left[-1+g\hbar k\right]f_{1}(r)\dfrac{k}{r^{2}}\left[{\rm i}\hbar\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}-(-{\rm i}\hbar\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\left[1-g\hbar k\right]f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right).

Because

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{\mathcal{E}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{-}\vec{\nabla}\cdot\left\{\left[\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+\dfrac{\partial f_{2}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}+g\hbar f_{1}(r)\dfrac{k}{r^{2}}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)\right]\vec{r}\right\}+\vec{\nabla}\cdot\left\{\left[-1+g\hbar k\right]f_{1}(r)\vec{S}\right\},

(182)

on one hand, we have

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\cdot\left\{\left[\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+\dfrac{\partial f_{2}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}+f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)\right]\vec{r}\right\}

(183)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial}{\partial x}\left\{\left[\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+\dfrac{\partial f_{2}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}+f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)\right]x\right\}

\displaystyle+\dfrac{\partial}{\partial y}\left\{\left[\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+\dfrac{\partial f_{2}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}+f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)\right]y\right\}

\displaystyle+\dfrac{\partial}{\partial z}\left\{\left[\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+\dfrac{\partial f_{2}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}+f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)\right]z\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+\dfrac{\partial f_{2}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}+f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)\right]\left(\dfrac{\partial x}{\partial x}+\dfrac{\partial y}{\partial y}+\dfrac{\partial z}{\partial z}\right)

\displaystyle+\dfrac{\partial}{\partial x}\left\{\left[\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+\dfrac{\partial f_{2}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}+f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)\right]\right\}x

\displaystyle+\dfrac{\partial}{\partial y}\left\{\left[\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+\dfrac{\partial f_{2}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}+f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)\right]\right\}y

\displaystyle+\dfrac{\partial}{\partial z}\left\{\left[\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+\dfrac{\partial f_{2}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}+f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)\right]\right\}z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 3\left[\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+\dfrac{\partial f_{2}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}+f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\left\{\left[\dfrac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}+f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\right)\right]\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+\left(\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}+f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\right)\dfrac{\partial\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)}{\partial x}+\dfrac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(\dfrac{\partial f_{2}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}\right)\right\}x

\displaystyle+\left\{\left[\dfrac{\partial}{\partial y}\left(\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}+f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\right)\right]\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+\left(\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}+f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\right)\dfrac{\partial\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)}{\partial y}+\dfrac{\partial}{\partial y}\left(\dfrac{\partial f_{2}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}\right)\right\}y

\displaystyle+\left\{\left[\dfrac{\partial}{\partial z}\left(\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}+f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\right)\right]\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+\left(\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}+f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\right)\dfrac{\partial\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)}{\partial z}+\dfrac{\partial}{\partial z}\left(\dfrac{\partial f_{2}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}\right)\right\}z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 3\left[\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+\dfrac{\partial f_{2}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}+f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\left\{\left[\dfrac{\partial}{\partial r}\left(\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}+f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\right)\right]\dfrac{x}{r}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+\left(\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}+f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\right)S_{x}+\dfrac{\partial}{\partial r}\left(\dfrac{\partial f_{2}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}\right)\dfrac{x}{r}\right\}x

\displaystyle+\left\{\left[\dfrac{\partial}{\partial r}\left(\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}+f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\right)\right]\dfrac{y}{r}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+\left(\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}+f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\right)S_{y}+\dfrac{\partial}{\partial r}\left(\dfrac{\partial f_{2}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}\right)\dfrac{y}{r}\right\}y

\displaystyle+\left\{\left[\dfrac{\partial}{\partial r}\left(\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}+f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\right)\right]\dfrac{z}{r}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+\left(\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}+f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\right)S_{z}+\dfrac{\partial}{\partial r}\left(\dfrac{\partial f_{2}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}\right)\dfrac{z}{r}\right\}z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 3\left[\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+\dfrac{\partial f_{2}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}+f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\left\{\left[\dfrac{\partial}{\partial r}\left(\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}+f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\right)\right]r\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+\left(\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}+f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\right)\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+\left[\dfrac{\partial}{\partial r}\left(\dfrac{\partial f_{2}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}\right)\right]r\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 3\left[\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+\dfrac{\partial f_{2}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}+f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\Biggr\{{\left[\left(\dfrac{\partial^{2}f_{1}(r)}{\partial r^{2}}-\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}+\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r}-2f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\right)\right]\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+\left(\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}+f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\right)\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)}

\displaystyle+\dfrac{\partial^{2}f_{2}(r)}{\partial r^{2}}-\dfrac{\partial f_{2}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}\Biggr\},

i.e.,

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\cdot\left\{\left[\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+\dfrac{\partial f_{2}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}+f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)\right]\vec{r}\right\}

(184)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 3\left[\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\Biggr\{\left[\left(\dfrac{\partial^{2}f_{1}(r)}{\partial r^{2}}+\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r}-2f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\right)\right]\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+\left(f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\right)\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+\dfrac{\partial^{2}f_{2}(r)}{\partial r^{2}}+2\dfrac{\partial f_{2}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}\Biggr\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\left[\left(\dfrac{\partial^{2}f_{1}(r)}{\partial r^{2}}+\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{3+g\hbar k}{r}+2f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\right)\right]\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+\left[\dfrac{\partial^{2}f_{2}(r)}{\partial r^{2}}+2\dfrac{\partial f_{2}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}\right].}

On the other hand, we can have

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\cdot\left\{\left[-1+g\hbar k\right]f_{1}(r)\vec{S}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial}{\partial x}\left\{\left[-1+g\hbar k\right]f_{1}(r)S_{x}\right\}+\dfrac{\partial}{\partial y}\left\{\left[-1+g\hbar k\right]f_{1}(r)S_{y}\right\}+\dfrac{\partial}{\partial z}\left\{\left[-1+g\hbar k\right]f_{1}(r)S_{z}\right\}

(185)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(-1+g\hbar k\right)\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial x}S_{x}+\left(-1+g\hbar k\right)\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial y}S_{y}+\left(-1+g\hbar k\right)\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial z}S_{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(-1+g\hbar k\right)\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{x}{r}S_{x}+\left(-1+g\hbar k\right)\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{y}{r}S_{y}+\left(-1+g\hbar k\right)\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{z}{r}S_{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(-1+g\hbar k\right)\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left(1-g\hbar k\right)\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right).

Therefore, from Eq. (
11a
) we have

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{\mathcal{E}}\ +{{\rm i}\,g\Bigl(\vec{\mathcal{A}}\cdot\vec{\mathcal{E}}-\vec{\mathcal{E}}\cdot\vec{\mathcal{A}}\Bigr)}

(186)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left[\left(\dfrac{\partial^{2}f_{1}(r)}{\partial r^{2}}+\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{3+g\hbar k}{r}+2f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\right)\right]\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)-\left[\dfrac{\partial^{2}f_{2}(r)}{\partial r^{2}}+2\dfrac{\partial f_{2}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}\right]

\displaystyle-\left(1-g\hbar k\right)\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+2\left[1-g\hbar k\right]f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)=0,

i.e.,

\displaystyle\left[\left(\dfrac{\partial^{2}f_{1}(r)}{\partial r^{2}}+\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{3+g\hbar k}{r}+2f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\right)\right]\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+\left[\dfrac{\partial^{2}f_{2}(r)}{\partial r^{2}}+2\dfrac{\partial f_{2}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}\right]

\displaystyle+\left(1-g\hbar k\right)\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)-2\left[1-g\hbar k\right]f_{1}(r)\dfrac{g\hbar k}{r^{2}}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)=0,

(187)

i.e.,

\displaystyle\left[\dfrac{\partial^{2}f_{1}(r)}{\partial r^{2}}+\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{4}{r}+2f_{1}(r)\dfrac{(g\hbar k)^{2}}{r^{2}}\right]\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+\left[\dfrac{\partial^{2}f_{2}(r)}{\partial r^{2}}+2\dfrac{\partial f_{2}(r)}{\partial r}\dfrac{1}{r}\right]=0,

(188)

i.e.,

\displaystyle\left[\dfrac{\partial^{2}f_{1}(r)}{\partial r^{2}}+\dfrac{4}{r}\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}+2\dfrac{(g\hbar k)^{2}}{r^{2}}f_{1}(r)\right]\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)+\left[\dfrac{\partial^{2}f_{2}(r)}{\partial r^{2}}+\dfrac{2}{r}\dfrac{\partial f_{2}(r)}{\partial r}\right]=0.

(189)

Based on above, Eq. (
11a
) yields the following conditions for two functions
f_{1}(r)
and
f_{2}(r)
:

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial^{2}f_{1}(r)}{\partial r^{2}}+\dfrac{4}{r}\dfrac{\partial f_{1}(r)}{\partial r}+2\dfrac{(g\hbar k)^{2}}{r^{2}}f_{1}(r)=0,

(190a)

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial^{2}f_{2}(r)}{\partial r^{2}}+\dfrac{2}{r}\dfrac{\partial f_{2}(r)}{\partial r}=0,

(190b)

whose solutions are

\displaystyle f_{1}(r)=\kappa_{1}r^{\nu_{1}}+\kappa_{2}r^{\nu_{2}},

\displaystyle f_{2}(r)=\dfrac{\kappa_{3}}{r}+\kappa_{4},

(191)

with

\displaystyle\nu_{1}=\frac{1}{2}\left(1-a-\sqrt{(a-1)^{2}-4b}\right),\;\;\;\;\nu_{2}=\frac{1}{2}\left(1-a+\sqrt{(a-1)^{2}-4b}\right),

\displaystyle a=4,\;\;\;\;b=2(g\hbar k)^{2}.

(192)

Specially, if
k=0
, then
a=4,b=0
, then
\nu_{1}=-3,\nu_{2}=0
.

Analysis 2: The study of Eq. (
11c
)—
Because

\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{\mathcal{A}}=-2k\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}\vec{r},

(193)

\displaystyle\vec{\mathcal{A}}\times\vec{\mathcal{A}}={\rm i}\hbar\;k^{2}\;\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}\;\vec{r},

(194)

we have

\displaystyle\vec{\mathcal{B}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{\mathcal{A}}-{\rm i}\,g\bigl(\vec{\mathcal{A}}\times\vec{\mathcal{A}}\bigr)

(195)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2k\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}\vec{r}-{\rm i}\,g\left[{\rm i}\hbar\;k^{2}\;\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}\;\vec{r}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2k\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}\vec{r}+g\hbar\;k^{2}\;\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}\;\vec{r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-2+g\hbar k)k\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}\vec{r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(2-g\hbar k)k\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}\vec{r}.

Then we have

\displaystyle{\rm i}\,g\Bigl(\vec{\mathcal{A}}\cdot\vec{\mathcal{B}}-\vec{\mathcal{B}}\cdot\vec{\mathcal{A}}\Bigr)=0,

(196)

and

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{\mathcal{B}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(2-g\hbar k)k\vec{\nabla}\cdot\left[\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}\vec{r}\right]

(197)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(2-g\hbar k)k\left\{\dfrac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}x\right)+\dfrac{\partial}{\partial y}\left(\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}y\right)+\dfrac{\partial}{\partial z}\left(\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}z\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(2-g\hbar k)k\left\{\left[\dfrac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}\right)\right]x+\left[\dfrac{\partial}{\partial y}\left(\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}\right)\right]y+\left[\dfrac{\partial}{\partial z}\left(\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}\right)\right]z+3\left(\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(2-g\hbar k)k\left\{\left[\dfrac{\partial}{\partial r}\left(\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}\right)\right]\dfrac{x^{2}}{r}+\left[\dfrac{\partial}{\partial r}\left(\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}\right)\right]\dfrac{y^{2}}{r}+\left[\dfrac{\partial}{\partial r}\left(\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}\right)\right]\dfrac{z^{2}}{r}+3\left(\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(2-g\hbar k)k\left\{\left[\dfrac{\partial}{\partial r}\left(\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}\right)\right]r+3\left(\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=
